========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hello, Doctor. It's, uh, nice to meet you too. I... I suppose I should just start from the beginning. I came in because, well, my mind just feels incredibly foggy lately. It's like I'm trying to think through a thick bowl of oatmeal, you know? I've been a library assistant for years, doing clerical work, mostly cataloguing, which I usually enjoy. But lately, I cannot even keep my place in my old cataloguing notes. I'll be looking right at a stack of books, books I've handled a dozen times, and I just stare at them. I can't even decide what simple thing to do next. Should I sort by author? By Dewey Decimal? It's like the gears in my head are just... stuck. I'll sit there for twenty minutes trying to figure out if I should put the new arrivals on the cart or stamp the due date cards. It's frustrating, really, because it's not hard work, it's just... I can't seem to process the steps anymore. And it's not just at work, it's everywhere, this... this slowness, this inability to just think clearly and make a simple choice.

Well, yes... yes, I suppose 'down' is one word for it, though it feels almost... well, heavier than just being down. Most days lately, I don't even really know where the morning goes. I'll just sit there at the kitchen table—it’s right by this little window that usually gets such nice light, you know, but lately it doesn't even seem to matter if the sun is shining or not. I just sit there and it honestly feels like the color has just completely gone out of everything. Just... drained right away, like an old photograph fading. 

I have these old watercolor books, beautiful things, really, books I’ve collected over the years from estate sales and library discard piles. I used to love leafing through them with my morning coffee, looking at the washes and the brush techniques. It was a nice way to start the day. But now... well, I’ll pull one toward me on the table, and instead of feeling interested or inspired like I used to, I just feel so... heavy. It’s this deep, sinking heaviness right in my chest. I just stare at the pages and it all looks so dull, and I feel so weighed down just looking at them, so I just leave them sitting there next to my mug for hours. It's just a very grey way to live, day in and day out.

Well, yes, I suppose I just touched on that, didn't I? With the watercolor books. But it's not just the books. It's... it's really everything. I used to take such pleasure in my little routines. Like my garden, for instance. I have this small patch out back, mostly hostas and ferns, shade plants, you know, because of the big oak tree. I used to spend my weekends out there, pruning, checking the soil, just enjoying the quiet of it all. It was peaceful. I’d even talk to the plants sometimes, silly as that sounds. But now? I look out the window and I just see chores. I see weeds that need pulling and I just... I can't bring myself to care. The thought of putting on my gardening gloves just feels exhausting. 

And my Sunday crosswords! I've been doing the Sunday paper crossword for, oh, it must be thirty years now. I even have a special dictionary I keep right next to my favorite armchair just for the tricky clues. I used to look forward to it all week, the challenge of it, the satisfaction of filling in that last little square. But the last few weeks, the paper just sits there on the porch. I don't even bother to take it out of the plastic sleeve. I just look at it and think, what's the point? It's just empty boxes. It’s like the things that used to give me a little spark, a little bit of joy, they just... they don't do anything for me anymore. It’s all just flat. Everything is just so incredibly flat.

But now... well, I’ll pull one toward me on the table, and instead of feeling interested or inspired like I used to, I just feel so... heavy. It’s this deep, sinking heaviness right in my chest. I just stare at the pages and it all looks so dull, and I feel so weighed down just looking at them, so I just leave them sitting there next to my mug for hours. It's just a very grey way to live, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I just touched on that, didn't I? With the watercolor books. But it's not just the books. It's... it's really everything. I used to take such pleasure in my little routines. Like my garden, for instance. I have this small patch out back, mostly hostas and ferns, shade plants, you know, because of the big oak tree. I used to spend my weekends out there, pruning, checking the soil, just enjoying the quiet of it all. It was peaceful. I’d even talk to the plants sometimes, silly as that sounds. But now? I look out the window and I just see chores. I see weeds that need pulling and I just... I can't bring myself to care. The thought of putting on my gardening gloves just feels exhausting. \n\nAnd my Sunday crosswords! I've been doing the Sunday paper crossword for, oh, it must be thirty years now. I even have a special dictionary I keep right next to my favorite armchair just for the tricky clues. I used to look forward to it all week, the challenge of it, the satisfaction of filling in that last little square. But the last few weeks, the paper just sits there on the porch. I don't even bother to take it out of the plastic sleeve. I just look at it and think, what's the point? It's just empty boxes. It’s like the things that used to give me a little spark, a little bit of joy, they just... they don't do anything for me anymore. It’s all just flat. Everything is just so incredibly flat.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Worthless? Well, no, I... I don't think I'd use that word, exactly. I mean, I know I have worth. I've always been a very steady sort of person, you know? I've worked at the county library branch for, gosh, it will be twenty-two years this coming October. And I know they rely on me. Well, they usually rely on me, before my brain turned to mush like I was saying earlier. But I know my value as a person. I'm a good neighbor, I always make sure the snow is shoveled on Mrs. Gable's side of the duplex—she's eighty-four, you see, and can't manage the heavy wet snow we get around here. So, I know I contribute. I don't sit around thinking I'm a bad person or a waste of space or anything severe like that. 

It's really more just... well, it's like I was saying about the flatness. I feel empty, maybe, or useless in the moment because I can't seem to get my chores done or sort my catalog cards, but not worthless in the grand scheme of things. Does that make sense? I'll sit in my armchair and think about my life, and I know I've done alright for a guy with just a high school diploma who never really went off to do big, grand, exciting things. I've lived a quiet life, but a very decent one. I don't hate myself. I don't have those kinds of dark thoughts about my character. It's just... I just don't have the energy to be the person I know I am right now. So, no, not worthless. Just... stalled out, I suppose you could say.

I just stare at the pages and it all looks so dull, and I feel so weighed down just looking at them, so I just leave them sitting there next to my mug for hours. It's just a very grey way to live, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I just touched on that, didn't I? With the watercolor books. But it's not just the books. It's... it's really everything. I used to take such pleasure in my little routines. Like my garden, for instance. I have this small patch out back, mostly hostas and ferns, shade plants, you know, because of the big oak tree. I used to spend my weekends out there, pruning, checking the soil, just enjoying the quiet of it all. It was peaceful. I’d even talk to the plants sometimes, silly as that sounds. But now? I look out the window and I just see chores. I see weeds that need pulling and I just... I can't bring myself to care. The thought of putting on my gardening gloves just feels exhausting. \n\nAnd my Sunday crosswords! I've been doing the Sunday paper crossword for, oh, it must be thirty years now. I even have a special dictionary I keep right next to my favorite armchair just for the tricky clues. I used to look forward to it all week, the challenge of it, the satisfaction of filling in that last little square. But the last few weeks, the paper just sits there on the porch. I don't even bother to take it out of the plastic sleeve. I just look at it and think, what's the point? It's just empty boxes. It’s like the things that used to give me a little spark, a little bit of joy, they just... they don't do anything for me anymore. It’s all just flat. Everything is just so incredibly flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless? Well, no, I... I don't think I'd use that word, exactly. I mean, I know I have worth. I've always been a very steady sort of person, you know? I've worked at the county library branch for, gosh, it will be twenty-two years this coming October. And I know they rely on me. Well, they usually rely on me, before my brain turned to mush like I was saying earlier. But I know my value as a person. I'm a good neighbor, I always make sure the snow is shoveled on Mrs. Gable's side of the duplex—she's eighty-four, you see, and can't manage the heavy wet snow we get around here. So, I know I contribute. I don't sit around thinking I'm a bad person or a waste of space or anything severe like that. \n\nIt's really more just... well, it's like I was saying about the flatness. I feel empty, maybe, or useless in the moment because I can't seem to get my chores done or sort my catalog cards, but not worthless in the grand scheme of things. Does that make sense? I'll sit in my armchair and think about my life, and I know I've done alright for a guy with just a high school diploma who never really went off to do big, grand, exciting things. I've lived a quiet life, but a very decent one. I don't hate myself. I don't have those kinds of dark thoughts about my character. It's just... I just don't have the energy to be the person I know I am right now. So, no, not worthless. Just... stalled out, I suppose you could say.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well, gosh, before this? No, I really don't think so. I mean, life has its normal ups and downs, doesn't it? You know, you have a bad week here or there. Like back in, oh, it must have been 2015, when they restructured the county library system and I thought I might be transferred to the downtown branch. I was terribly worried about the commute—the parking situation down there is just atrocious, you have to pay by the hour or walk six blocks from the municipal lot—and I remember I didn't sleep well for a few days, felt a bit low and anxious about the whole ordeal. But nothing like this. 

This... this heavy, gray blanket that just sits on me day after day, week after week... no, this is entirely new territory for me. I've always prided myself on being a very even-keeled sort of fellow. You know, steady. I show up, I do my work, I tend my hostas. Sure, maybe I've had a day or two of feeling under the weather or a bit blue in the winter months when the sun sets at four-thirty in the afternoon. I've always hated that, locking up the branch and driving home in the pitch dark, it makes the days feel so short. But two whole weeks of just... nothingness? Of not even wanting to look at my watercolors or do the Sunday puzzle? No, doctor. Never anything quite like this. 

It's frankly a bit frightening, to be honest with you, because I'm just not used to my mind feeling so... so stuck. I keep waiting to just snap out of it, like you would a common cold. I keep telling myself, 'Tomorrow morning, you'll wake up and make your coffee and want to read your book,' but then tomorrow comes and it's just the same flat feeling all over again. It just keeps dragging on. So, no, to answer your question, this is really the first time it's been this bad for this long.

Of not even wanting to look at my watercolors or do the Sunday puzzle? No, doctor. Never anything quite like this. \n\nIt's frankly a bit frightening, to be honest with you, because I'm just not used to my mind feeling so... so stuck. I keep waiting to just snap out of it, like you would a common cold. I keep telling myself, 'Tomorrow morning, you'll wake up and make your coffee and want to read your book,' but then tomorrow comes and it's just the same flat feeling all over again. It just keeps dragging on. So, no, to answer your question, this is really the first time it's been this bad for this long.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sleep? Well, it has been quite frustrating, to be completely honest with you. I mean, by the time evening rolls around, I am just so terribly exhausted. That heavy, flat feeling I was telling you about, it just wears me right down to the bone. I go through my usual evening routine, you know, I make myself a cup of herbal tea—usually peppermint, I find chamomile a bit too earthy—and I make sure the deadbolt is locked, and I get into bed right around ten-thirty. And I am tired. I really am. I pull the covers up and I am just so ready to be asleep and escape the day. 

But then, it never fails, my eyes just snap wide open. I roll over and look at the digital clock on the nightstand—it is one of those older models with the bright red numbers, I have had it for absolute ages, since the nineties at least—and it is always right around four in the morning. Four-fifteen, three-fifty-eight, right in that dreadful, quiet pocket of the night. And that is it for me. Sleep is just entirely gone. 

I do not get up, though. It is far too early to start the coffee maker, and it is pitch black outside, so I just lie there. I lie there staring up at the shadow of the ceiling fan, and my mind just starts churning. Not fast, mind you, just a slow, heavy sort of turning over. I just lie there thinking about what I am supposed to do with myself now. I look ahead at the whole empty day stretching out in front of me, thinking about the hours I have to fill when I have absolutely no desire to do any of my usual things. It is a very long, very lonely time to be awake, just trapped there in the dark wondering how on earth I am going to get through another day of feeling this way. So yes, sleep has been very poor indeed.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello, Doctor. It's, uh, nice to meet you too. I... I suppose I should just start from the beginning. I came in because, well, my mind just feels incredibly foggy lately. It's like I'm trying to think through a thick bowl of oatmeal, you know? I've been a library assistant for years, doing clerical work, mostly cataloguing, which I usually enjoy. But lately, I cannot even keep my place in my old cataloguing notes. I'll be looking right at a stack of books, books I've handled a dozen times, and I just stare at them. I can't even decide what simple thing to do next. Should I sort by author? By Dewey Decimal? It's like the gears in my head are just... stuck. I'll sit there for twenty minutes trying to figure out if I should put the new arrivals on the cart or stamp the due date cards. It's frustrating, really, because it's not hard work, it's just... I can't seem to process the steps anymore. And it's not just at work, it's everywhere, this... this slowness, this inability to just think clearly and make a simple choice.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... yes, I suppose 'down' is one word for it, though it feels almost... well, heavier than just being down. Most days lately, I don't even really know where the morning goes. I'll just sit there at the kitchen table—it’s right by this little window that usually gets such nice light, you know, but lately it doesn't even seem to matter if the sun is shining or not. I just sit there and it honestly feels like the color has just completely gone out of everything. Just... drained right away, like an old photograph fading. \n\nI have these old watercolor books, beautiful things, really, books I’ve collected over the years from estate sales and library discard piles. I used to love leafing through them with my morning coffee, looking at the washes and the brush techniques. It was a nice way to start the day. But now... well, I’ll pull one toward me on the table, and instead of feeling interested or inspired like I used to, I just feel so... heavy. It’s this deep, sinking heaviness right in my chest. I just stare at the pages and it all looks so dull, and I feel so weighed down just looking at them, so I just leave them sitting there next to my mug for hours. It's just a very grey way to live, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I just touched on that, didn't I? With the watercolor books. But it's not just the books. It's... it's really everything. I used to take such pleasure in my little routines. Like my garden, for instance. I have this small patch out back, mostly hostas and ferns, shade plants, you know, because of the big oak tree. I used to spend my weekends out there, pruning, checking the soil, just enjoying the quiet of it all. It was peaceful. I’d even talk to the plants sometimes, silly as that sounds. But now? I look out the window and I just see chores. I see weeds that need pulling and I just... I can't bring myself to care. The thought of putting on my gardening gloves just feels exhausting. \n\nAnd my Sunday crosswords! I've been doing the Sunday paper crossword for, oh, it must be thirty years now. I even have a special dictionary I keep right next to my favorite armchair just for the tricky clues. I used to look forward to it all week, the challenge of it, the satisfaction of filling in that last little square. But the last few weeks, the paper just sits there on the porch. I don't even bother to take it out of the plastic sleeve. I just look at it and think, what's the point? It's just empty boxes. It’s like the things that used to give me a little spark, a little bit of joy, they just... they don't do anything for me anymore. It’s all just flat. Everything is just so incredibly flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless? Well, no, I... I don't think I'd use that word, exactly. I mean, I know I have worth. I've always been a very steady sort of person, you know? I've worked at the county library branch for, gosh, it will be twenty-two years this coming October. And I know they rely on me. Well, they usually rely on me, before my brain turned to mush like I was saying earlier. But I know my value as a person. I'm a good neighbor, I always make sure the snow is shoveled on Mrs. Gable's side of the duplex—she's eighty-four, you see, and can't manage the heavy wet snow we get around here. So, I know I contribute. I don't sit around thinking I'm a bad person or a waste of space or anything severe like that. \n\nIt's really more just... well, it's like I was saying about the flatness. I feel empty, maybe, or useless in the moment because I can't seem to get my chores done or sort my catalog cards, but not worthless in the grand scheme of things. Does that make sense? I'll sit in my armchair and think about my life, and I know I've done alright for a guy with just a high school diploma who never really went off to do big, grand, exciting things. I've lived a quiet life, but a very decent one. I don't hate myself. I don't have those kinds of dark thoughts about my character. It's just... I just don't have the energy to be the person I know I am right now. So, no, not worthless. Just... stalled out, I suppose you could say.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, gosh, before this? No, I really don't think so. I mean, life has its normal ups and downs, doesn't it? You know, you have a bad week here or there. Like back in, oh, it must have been 2015, when they restructured the county library system and I thought I might be transferred to the downtown branch. I was terribly worried about the commute—the parking situation down there is just atrocious, you have to pay by the hour or walk six blocks from the municipal lot—and I remember I didn't sleep well for a few days, felt a bit low and anxious about the whole ordeal. But nothing like this. \n\nThis... this heavy, gray blanket that just sits on me day after day, week after week... no, this is entirely new territory for me. I've always prided myself on being a very even-keeled sort of fellow. You know, steady. I show up, I do my work, I tend my hostas. Sure, maybe I've had a day or two of feeling under the weather or a bit blue in the winter months when the sun sets at four-thirty in the afternoon. I've always hated that, locking up the branch and driving home in the pitch dark, it makes the days feel so short. But two whole weeks of just... nothingness? Of not even wanting to look at my watercolors or do the Sunday puzzle? No, doctor. Never anything quite like this. \n\nIt's frankly a bit frightening, to be honest with you, because I'm just not used to my mind feeling so... so stuck. I keep waiting to just snap out of it, like you would a common cold. I keep telling myself, 'Tomorrow morning, you'll wake up and make your coffee and want to read your book,' but then tomorrow comes and it's just the same flat feeling all over again. It just keeps dragging on. So, no, to answer your question, this is really the first time it's been this bad for this long.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Well, it has been quite frustrating, to be completely honest with you. I mean, by the time evening rolls around, I am just so terribly exhausted. That heavy, flat feeling I was telling you about, it just wears me right down to the bone. I go through my usual evening routine, you know, I make myself a cup of herbal tea—usually peppermint, I find chamomile a bit too earthy—and I make sure the deadbolt is locked, and I get into bed right around ten-thirty. And I am tired. I really am. I pull the covers up and I am just so ready to be asleep and escape the day. \n\nBut then, it never fails, my eyes just snap wide open. I roll over and look at the digital clock on the nightstand—it is one of those older models with the bright red numbers, I have had it for absolute ages, since the nineties at least—and it is always right around four in the morning. Four-fifteen, three-fifty-eight, right in that dreadful, quiet pocket of the night. And that is it for me. Sleep is just entirely gone. \n\nI do not get up, though. It is far too early to start the coffee maker, and it is pitch black outside, so I just lie there. I lie there staring up at the shadow of the ceiling fan, and my mind just starts churning. Not fast, mind you, just a slow, heavy sort of turning over. I just lie there thinking about what I am supposed to do with myself now. I look ahead at the whole empty day stretching out in front of me, thinking about the hours I have to fill when I have absolutely no desire to do any of my usual things. It is a very long, very lonely time to be awake, just trapped there in the dark wondering how on earth I am going to get through another day of feeling this way. So yes, sleep has been very poor indeed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I look ahead at the whole empty day stretching out in front of me, thinking about the hours I have to fill when I have absolutely no desire to do any of my usual things. It is a very long, very lonely time to be awake, just trapped there in the dark wondering how on earth I am going to get through another day of feeling this way. So yes, sleep has been very poor indeed.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you had thoughts that life is not worth living?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Life not worth living? Oh, gosh, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I know things are very difficult right now, and I was just telling you about those awful mornings lying awake at four a.m., dreading the day. But that's just because the days feel so long and heavy right now, not because I want to give up on life itself. 

I actually have a lot of respect for life, you know? I think about my parents—they passed away some time ago, my mother in 2008 and my father a few years before that—and they worked so hard to give me a good, stable upbringing. They went through the Depression and all sorts of hardships, and they always taught me to value what you have, even when things are tough. So, I would never just throw that away. 

And besides, I have my hostas to tend to when spring comes around, and Mrs. Gable next door, she really does rely on me, even if it's just to bring her mail up to the porch when the steps get icy. I know my life isn't some big, dramatic adventure, but it's mine, and I've built a comfortable little corner for myself here. I just want to feel like myself again in it, you see? I just want this gray fog to lift so I can go back to enjoying my peppermint tea and my Sunday puzzles. So, no, doctor, I don't have thoughts like that. I just want to feel better, not... not end things. That's not me at all.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Medical illness or injury? Oh, no, no. I've been quite fortunate in that regard, thankfully. I mean, my health has always been relatively sturdy. I haven't been to the hospital for anything serious since... oh, gosh, it must have been the late nineties when I had a rather nasty bout of kidney stones. Now, that was an ordeal. I wouldn't wish that on my worst enemy, let me tell you. I remember I was at the library, actually, sorting through some new acquisitions in the reference section, and this pain just hit me out of nowhere. I thought it was my back at first, you know, from lifting those heavy encyclopedias, but it just kept getting worse and worse. I had to have my supervisor, Brenda—she's retired now, moved down to Florida with her sister—drive me to the emergency room. 

But recently? No, nothing like that at all. I haven't even had a bad cold this winter, which is surprising because usually, the library is just a breeding ground for germs. All those people coming in and out, coughing on the keyboards, bringing in books they've had sitting on their nightstands while they were sick with the flu... you really have to be careful. I always make sure to wash my hands thoroughly, humming the 'Happy Birthday' song twice, just like they say. And I take my daily multivitamin, the one with the extra D3, because, as I mentioned, those dark winter evenings can really take a toll. 

I did have a bit of a scare last month, though, just a minor one. I was out back, putting down some extra mulch around the hostas before the first frost hit—I use a cedar blend, it really helps keep the moisture in and deters those awful slugs—and I stepped on a rake I had foolishly left lying in the grass. It popped right up and smacked me on the shin. Left a bruise the size of a saucer, all purple and yellow. It throbbed for a few days, especially when I was climbing the stairs to the second floor of the branch, but it faded eventually. Just a silly, clumsy mistake, really. Certainly nothing that required a doctor's attention. So, no, physically, aside from this terrible fatigue and the sleep issues, I've been completely fine. No recent illnesses, no injuries to speak of. Just... just this awful, heavy feeling in my mind.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.2, F32.8, F06.7, F06.8, F09\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello, Doctor. It's, uh, nice to meet you too. I... I suppose I should just start from the beginning. I came in because, well, my mind just feels incredibly foggy lately. It's like I'm trying to think through a thick bowl of oatmeal, you know? I've been a library assistant for years, doing clerical work, mostly cataloguing, which I usually enjoy. But lately, I cannot even keep my place in my old cataloguing notes. I'll be looking right at a stack of books, books I've handled a dozen times, and I just stare at them. I can't even decide what simple thing to do next. Should I sort by author? By Dewey Decimal? It's like the gears in my head are just... stuck. I'll sit there for twenty minutes trying to figure out if I should put the new arrivals on the cart or stamp the due date cards. It's frustrating, really, because it's not hard work, it's just... I can't seem to process the steps anymore. And it's not just at work, it's everywhere, this... this slowness, this inability to just think clearly and make a simple choice.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... yes, I suppose 'down' is one word for it, though it feels almost... well, heavier than just being down. Most days lately, I don't even really know where the morning goes. I'll just sit there at the kitchen table—it’s right by this little window that usually gets such nice light, you know, but lately it doesn't even seem to matter if the sun is shining or not. I just sit there and it honestly feels like the color has just completely gone out of everything. Just... drained right away, like an old photograph fading. \n\nI have these old watercolor books, beautiful things, really, books I’ve collected over the years from estate sales and library discard piles. I used to love leafing through them with my morning coffee, looking at the washes and the brush techniques. It was a nice way to start the day. But now... well, I’ll pull one toward me on the table, and instead of feeling interested or inspired like I used to, I just feel so... heavy. It’s this deep, sinking heaviness right in my chest. I just stare at the pages and it all looks so dull, and I feel so weighed down just looking at them, so I just leave them sitting there next to my mug for hours. It's just a very grey way to live, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I just touched on that, didn't I? With the watercolor books. But it's not just the books. It's... it's really everything. I used to take such pleasure in my little routines. Like my garden, for instance. I have this small patch out back, mostly hostas and ferns, shade plants, you know, because of the big oak tree. I used to spend my weekends out there, pruning, checking the soil, just enjoying the quiet of it all. It was peaceful. I’d even talk to the plants sometimes, silly as that sounds. But now? I look out the window and I just see chores. I see weeds that need pulling and I just... I can't bring myself to care. The thought of putting on my gardening gloves just feels exhausting. \n\nAnd my Sunday crosswords! I've been doing the Sunday paper crossword for, oh, it must be thirty years now. I even have a special dictionary I keep right next to my favorite armchair just for the tricky clues. I used to look forward to it all week, the challenge of it, the satisfaction of filling in that last little square. But the last few weeks, the paper just sits there on the porch. I don't even bother to take it out of the plastic sleeve. I just look at it and think, what's the point? It's just empty boxes. It’s like the things that used to give me a little spark, a little bit of joy, they just... they don't do anything for me anymore. It’s all just flat. Everything is just so incredibly flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless? Well, no, I... I don't think I'd use that word, exactly. I mean, I know I have worth. I've always been a very steady sort of person, you know? I've worked at the county library branch for, gosh, it will be twenty-two years this coming October. And I know they rely on me. Well, they usually rely on me, before my brain turned to mush like I was saying earlier. But I know my value as a person. I'm a good neighbor, I always make sure the snow is shoveled on Mrs. Gable's side of the duplex—she's eighty-four, you see, and can't manage the heavy wet snow we get around here. So, I know I contribute. I don't sit around thinking I'm a bad person or a waste of space or anything severe like that. \n\nIt's really more just... well, it's like I was saying about the flatness. I feel empty, maybe, or useless in the moment because I can't seem to get my chores done or sort my catalog cards, but not worthless in the grand scheme of things. Does that make sense? I'll sit in my armchair and think about my life, and I know I've done alright for a guy with just a high school diploma who never really went off to do big, grand, exciting things. I've lived a quiet life, but a very decent one. I don't hate myself. I don't have those kinds of dark thoughts about my character. It's just... I just don't have the energy to be the person I know I am right now. So, no, not worthless. Just... stalled out, I suppose you could say.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, gosh, before this? No, I really don't think so. I mean, life has its normal ups and downs, doesn't it? You know, you have a bad week here or there. Like back in, oh, it must have been 2015, when they restructured the county library system and I thought I might be transferred to the downtown branch. I was terribly worried about the commute—the parking situation down there is just atrocious, you have to pay by the hour or walk six blocks from the municipal lot—and I remember I didn't sleep well for a few days, felt a bit low and anxious about the whole ordeal. But nothing like this. \n\nThis... this heavy, gray blanket that just sits on me day after day, week after week... no, this is entirely new territory for me. I've always prided myself on being a very even-keeled sort of fellow. You know, steady. I show up, I do my work, I tend my hostas. Sure, maybe I've had a day or two of feeling under the weather or a bit blue in the winter months when the sun sets at four-thirty in the afternoon. I've always hated that, locking up the branch and driving home in the pitch dark, it makes the days feel so short. But two whole weeks of just... nothingness? Of not even wanting to look at my watercolors or do the Sunday puzzle? No, doctor. Never anything quite like this. \n\nIt's frankly a bit frightening, to be honest with you, because I'm just not used to my mind feeling so... so stuck. I keep waiting to just snap out of it, like you would a common cold. I keep telling myself, 'Tomorrow morning, you'll wake up and make your coffee and want to read your book,' but then tomorrow comes and it's just the same flat feeling all over again. It just keeps dragging on. So, no, to answer your question, this is really the first time it's been this bad for this long.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Well, it has been quite frustrating, to be completely honest with you. I mean, by the time evening rolls around, I am just so terribly exhausted. That heavy, flat feeling I was telling you about, it just wears me right down to the bone. I go through my usual evening routine, you know, I make myself a cup of herbal tea—usually peppermint, I find chamomile a bit too earthy—and I make sure the deadbolt is locked, and I get into bed right around ten-thirty. And I am tired. I really am. I pull the covers up and I am just so ready to be asleep and escape the day. \n\nBut then, it never fails, my eyes just snap wide open. I roll over and look at the digital clock on the nightstand—it is one of those older models with the bright red numbers, I have had it for absolute ages, since the nineties at least—and it is always right around four in the morning. Four-fifteen, three-fifty-eight, right in that dreadful, quiet pocket of the night. And that is it for me. Sleep is just entirely gone. \n\nI do not get up, though. It is far too early to start the coffee maker, and it is pitch black outside, so I just lie there. I lie there staring up at the shadow of the ceiling fan, and my mind just starts churning. Not fast, mind you, just a slow, heavy sort of turning over. I just lie there thinking about what I am supposed to do with myself now. I look ahead at the whole empty day stretching out in front of me, thinking about the hours I have to fill when I have absolutely no desire to do any of my usual things. It is a very long, very lonely time to be awake, just trapped there in the dark wondering how on earth I am going to get through another day of feeling this way. So yes, sleep has been very poor indeed.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: Life not worth living? Oh, gosh, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I know things are very difficult right now, and I was just telling you about those awful mornings lying awake at four a.m., dreading the day. But that's just because the days feel so long and heavy right now, not because I want to give up on life itself. \n\nI actually have a lot of respect for life, you know? I think about my parents—they passed away some time ago, my mother in 2008 and my father a few years before that—and they worked so hard to give me a good, stable upbringing. They went through the Depression and all sorts of hardships, and they always taught me to value what you have, even when things are tough. So, I would never just throw that away. \n\nAnd besides, I have my hostas to tend to when spring comes around, and Mrs. Gable next door, she really does rely on me, even if it's just to bring her mail up to the porch when the steps get icy. I know my life isn't some big, dramatic adventure, but it's mine, and I've built a comfortable little corner for myself here. I just want to feel like myself again in it, you see? I just want this gray fog to lift so I can go back to enjoying my peppermint tea and my Sunday puzzles. So, no, doctor, I don't have thoughts like that. I just want to feel better, not... not end things. That's not me at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent medical illness or injury?\n\nPatient: Medical illness or injury? Oh, no, no. I've been quite fortunate in that regard, thankfully. I mean, my health has always been relatively sturdy. I haven't been to the hospital for anything serious since... oh, gosh, it must have been the late nineties when I had a rather nasty bout of kidney stones. Now, that was an ordeal. I wouldn't wish that on my worst enemy, let me tell you. I remember I was at the library, actually, sorting through some new acquisitions in the reference section, and this pain just hit me out of nowhere. I thought it was my back at first, you know, from lifting those heavy encyclopedias, but it just kept getting worse and worse. I had to have my supervisor, Brenda—she's retired now, moved down to Florida with her sister—drive me to the emergency room. \n\nBut recently? No, nothing like that at all. I haven't even had a bad cold this winter, which is surprising because usually, the library is just a breeding ground for germs. All those people coming in and out, coughing on the keyboards, bringing in books they've had sitting on their nightstands while they were sick with the flu... you really have to be careful. I always make sure to wash my hands thoroughly, humming the 'Happy Birthday' song twice, just like they say. And I take my daily multivitamin, the one with the extra D3, because, as I mentioned, those dark winter evenings can really take a toll. \n\nI did have a bit of a scare last month, though, just a minor one. I was out back, putting down some extra mulch around the hostas before the first frost hit—I use a cedar blend, it really helps keep the moisture in and deters those awful slugs—and I stepped on a rake I had foolishly left lying in the grass. It popped right up and smacked me on the shin. Left a bruise the size of a saucer, all purple and yellow. It throbbed for a few days, especially when I was climbing the stairs to the second floor of the branch, but it faded eventually. Just a silly, clumsy mistake, really. Certainly nothing that required a doctor's attention. So, no, physically, aside from this terrible fatigue and the sleep issues, I've been completely fine. No recent illnesses, no injuries to speak of. Just... just this awful, heavy feeling in my mind.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I haven't been to the hospital for anything serious since... oh, gosh, it must have been the late nineties when I had a rather nasty bout of kidney stones. Now, that was an ordeal. I wouldn't wish that on my worst enemy, let me tell you. I remember I was at the library, actually, sorting through some new acquisitions in the reference section, and this pain just hit me out of nowhere. I thought it was my back at first, you know, from lifting those heavy encyclopedias, but it just kept getting worse and worse. I had to have my supervisor, Brenda—she's retired now, moved down to Florida with her sister—drive me to the emergency room. \n\nBut recently? No, nothing like that at all. I haven't even had a bad cold this winter, which is surprising because usually, the library is just a breeding ground for germs. All those people coming in and out, coughing on the keyboards, bringing in books they've had sitting on their nightstands while they were sick with the flu... you really have to be careful. I always make sure to wash my hands thoroughly, humming the 'Happy Birthday' song twice, just like they say. And I take my daily multivitamin, the one with the extra D3, because, as I mentioned, those dark winter evenings can really take a toll. \n\nI did have a bit of a scare last month, though, just a minor one. I was out back, putting down some extra mulch around the hostas before the first frost hit—I use a cedar blend, it really helps keep the moisture in and deters those awful slugs—and I stepped on a rake I had foolishly left lying in the grass. It popped right up and smacked me on the shin. Left a bruise the size of a saucer, all purple and yellow. It throbbed for a few days, especially when I was climbing the stairs to the second floor of the branch, but it faded eventually. Just a silly, clumsy mistake, really. Certainly nothing that required a doctor's attention. So, no, physically, aside from this terrible fatigue and the sleep issues, I've been completely fine. No recent illnesses, no injuries to speak of. Just... just this awful, heavy feeling in my mind.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you started any new medications or supplements recently?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
New medications or supplements? Oh, no, nothing new at all. I am very particular about what I take, you know. I mentioned my multivitamin earlier, the one with the extra D3, and I've been taking that same brand for... oh, it must be fifteen years now. I buy it at the local pharmacy, the one on Elm Street, not those big chain stores. I like to support local businesses when I can, and Mr. Henderson, the pharmacist there, always makes sure they have it in stock for me. 

But as for anything new, no. I don't like to upset the apple cart, so to speak. My system is used to what it's used to. I mean, I see all those advertisements on the television, especially during the evening news, pushing this pill for that problem, and that powder for another thing... it's all just too much. I always think, who knows how all those things interact with each other? It just seems like asking for trouble. 

Even when I had that nasty bruise on my shin from the rake incident I told you about, I didn't even take an ibuprofen. I just put a cold compress on it—a bag of frozen peas wrapped in a clean dish towel, the blue and white checkered one, to be exact—and elevated my leg on the ottoman while I read. I prefer to let the body heal itself whenever possible, rather than throwing a bunch of new chemicals at it. So, to answer your question, no. My medicine cabinet looks exactly the same today as it did five years ago. Just the vitamins, some bandages, and a bottle of rubbing alcohol. Nothing new whatsoever.

I came in because, well, my mind just feels incredibly foggy lately. It's like I'm trying to think through a thick bowl of oatmeal, you know? I've been a library assistant for years, doing clerical work, mostly cataloguing, which I usually enjoy. But lately, I cannot even keep my place in my old cataloguing notes. I'll be looking right at a stack of books, books I've handled a dozen times, and I just stare at them. I can't even decide what simple thing to do next. Should I sort by author? By Dewey Decimal? It's like the gears in my head are just... stuck. I'll sit there for twenty minutes trying to figure out if I should put the new arrivals on the cart or stamp the due date cards. It's frustrating, really, because it's not hard work, it's just... I can't seem to process the steps anymore. And it's not just at work, it's everywhere, this... this slowness, this inability to just think clearly and make a simple choice.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Well, yes... yes, I suppose 'down' is one word for it, though it feels almost... well, heavier than just being down. Most days lately, I don't even really know where the morning goes. I'll just sit there at the kitchen table—it’s right by this little window that usually gets such nice light, you know, but lately it doesn't even seem to matter if the sun is shining or not. I just sit there and it honestly feels like the color has just completely gone out of everything. Just... drained right away, like an old photograph fading. \n\nI have these old watercolor books, beautiful things, really, books I’ve collected over the years from estate sales and library discard piles. I used to love leafing through them with my morning coffee, looking at the washes and the brush techniques. It was a nice way to start the day. But now... well, I’ll pull one toward me on the table, and instead of feeling interested or inspired like I used to, I just feel so... heavy. It’s this deep, sinking heaviness right in my chest. I just stare at the pages and it all looks so dull, and I feel so weighed down just looking at them, so I just leave them sitting there next to my mug for hours. It's just a very grey way to live, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose I just touched on that, didn't I? With the watercolor books. But it's not just the books. It's... it's really everything. I used to take such pleasure in my little routines. Like my garden, for instance. I have this small patch out back, mostly hostas and ferns, shade plants, you know, because of the big oak tree. I used to spend my weekends out there, pruning, checking the soil, just enjoying the quiet of it all. It was peaceful. I’d even talk to the plants sometimes, silly as that sounds. But now? I look out the window and I just see chores. I see weeds that need pulling and I just... I can't bring myself to care. The thought of putting on my gardening gloves just feels exhausting. \n\nAnd my Sunday crosswords! I've been doing the Sunday paper crossword for, oh, it must be thirty years now. I even have a special dictionary I keep right next to my favorite armchair just for the tricky clues. I used to look forward to it all week, the challenge of it, the satisfaction of filling in that last little square. But the last few weeks, the paper just sits there on the porch. I don't even bother to take it out of the plastic sleeve. I just look at it and think, what's the point? It's just empty boxes. It’s like the things that used to give me a little spark, a little bit of joy, they just... they don't do anything for me anymore. It’s all just flat. Everything is just so incredibly flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless? Well, no, I... I don't think I'd use that word, exactly. I mean, I know I have worth. I've always been a very steady sort of person, you know? I've worked at the county library branch for, gosh, it will be twenty-two years this coming October. And I know they rely on me. Well, they usually rely on me, before my brain turned to mush like I was saying earlier. But I know my value as a person. I'm a good neighbor, I always make sure the snow is shoveled on Mrs. Gable's side of the duplex—she's eighty-four, you see, and can't manage the heavy wet snow we get around here. So, I know I contribute. I don't sit around thinking I'm a bad person or a waste of space or anything severe like that. \n\nIt's really more just... well, it's like I was saying about the flatness. I feel empty, maybe, or useless in the moment because I can't seem to get my chores done or sort my catalog cards, but not worthless in the grand scheme of things. Does that make sense? I'll sit in my armchair and think about my life, and I know I've done alright for a guy with just a high school diploma who never really went off to do big, grand, exciting things. I've lived a quiet life, but a very decent one. I don't hate myself. I don't have those kinds of dark thoughts about my character. It's just... I just don't have the energy to be the person I know I am right now. So, no, not worthless. Just... stalled out, I suppose you could say.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, gosh, before this? No, I really don't think so. I mean, life has its normal ups and downs, doesn't it? You know, you have a bad week here or there. Like back in, oh, it must have been 2015, when they restructured the county library system and I thought I might be transferred to the downtown branch. I was terribly worried about the commute—the parking situation down there is just atrocious, you have to pay by the hour or walk six blocks from the municipal lot—and I remember I didn't sleep well for a few days, felt a bit low and anxious about the whole ordeal. But nothing like this. \n\nThis... this heavy, gray blanket that just sits on me day after day, week after week... no, this is entirely new territory for me. I've always prided myself on being a very even-keeled sort of fellow. You know, steady. I show up, I do my work, I tend my hostas. Sure, maybe I've had a day or two of feeling under the weather or a bit blue in the winter months when the sun sets at four-thirty in the afternoon. I've always hated that, locking up the branch and driving home in the pitch dark, it makes the days feel so short. But two whole weeks of just... nothingness? Of not even wanting to look at my watercolors or do the Sunday puzzle? No, doctor. Never anything quite like this. \n\nIt's frankly a bit frightening, to be honest with you, because I'm just not used to my mind feeling so... so stuck. I keep waiting to just snap out of it, like you would a common cold. I keep telling myself, 'Tomorrow morning, you'll wake up and make your coffee and want to read your book,' but then tomorrow comes and it's just the same flat feeling all over again. It just keeps dragging on. So, no, to answer your question, this is really the first time it's been this bad for this long.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Well, it has been quite frustrating, to be completely honest with you. I mean, by the time evening rolls around, I am just so terribly exhausted. That heavy, flat feeling I was telling you about, it just wears me right down to the bone. I go through my usual evening routine, you know, I make myself a cup of herbal tea—usually peppermint, I find chamomile a bit too earthy—and I make sure the deadbolt is locked, and I get into bed right around ten-thirty. And I am tired. I really am. I pull the covers up and I am just so ready to be asleep and escape the day. \n\nBut then, it never fails, my eyes just snap wide open. I roll over and look at the digital clock on the nightstand—it is one of those older models with the bright red numbers, I have had it for absolute ages, since the nineties at least—and it is always right around four in the morning. Four-fifteen, three-fifty-eight, right in that dreadful, quiet pocket of the night. And that is it for me. Sleep is just entirely gone. \n\nI do not get up, though. It is far too early to start the coffee maker, and it is pitch black outside, so I just lie there. I lie there staring up at the shadow of the ceiling fan, and my mind just starts churning. Not fast, mind you, just a slow, heavy sort of turning over. I just lie there thinking about what I am supposed to do with myself now. I look ahead at the whole empty day stretching out in front of me, thinking about the hours I have to fill when I have absolutely no desire to do any of my usual things. It is a very long, very lonely time to be awake, just trapped there in the dark wondering how on earth I am going to get through another day of feeling this way. So yes, sleep has been very poor indeed.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: Life not worth living? Oh, gosh, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I know things are very difficult right now, and I was just telling you about those awful mornings lying awake at four a.m., dreading the day. But that's just because the days feel so long and heavy right now, not because I want to give up on life itself. \n\nI actually have a lot of respect for life, you know? I think about my parents—they passed away some time ago, my mother in 2008 and my father a few years before that—and they worked so hard to give me a good, stable upbringing. They went through the Depression and all sorts of hardships, and they always taught me to value what you have, even when things are tough. So, I would never just throw that away. \n\nAnd besides, I have my hostas to tend to when spring comes around, and Mrs. Gable next door, she really does rely on me, even if it's just to bring her mail up to the porch when the steps get icy. I know my life isn't some big, dramatic adventure, but it's mine, and I've built a comfortable little corner for myself here. I just want to feel like myself again in it, you see? I just want this gray fog to lift so I can go back to enjoying my peppermint tea and my Sunday puzzles. So, no, doctor, I don't have thoughts like that. I just want to feel better, not... not end things. That's not me at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent medical illness or injury?\n\nPatient: Medical illness or injury? Oh, no, no. I've been quite fortunate in that regard, thankfully. I mean, my health has always been relatively sturdy. I haven't been to the hospital for anything serious since... oh, gosh, it must have been the late nineties when I had a rather nasty bout of kidney stones. Now, that was an ordeal. I wouldn't wish that on my worst enemy, let me tell you. I remember I was at the library, actually, sorting through some new acquisitions in the reference section, and this pain just hit me out of nowhere. I thought it was my back at first, you know, from lifting those heavy encyclopedias, but it just kept getting worse and worse. I had to have my supervisor, Brenda—she's retired now, moved down to Florida with her sister—drive me to the emergency room. \n\nBut recently? No, nothing like that at all. I haven't even had a bad cold this winter, which is surprising because usually, the library is just a breeding ground for germs. All those people coming in and out, coughing on the keyboards, bringing in books they've had sitting on their nightstands while they were sick with the flu... you really have to be careful. I always make sure to wash my hands thoroughly, humming the 'Happy Birthday' song twice, just like they say. And I take my daily multivitamin, the one with the extra D3, because, as I mentioned, those dark winter evenings can really take a toll. \n\nI did have a bit of a scare last month, though, just a minor one. I was out back, putting down some extra mulch around the hostas before the first frost hit—I use a cedar blend, it really helps keep the moisture in and deters those awful slugs—and I stepped on a rake I had foolishly left lying in the grass. It popped right up and smacked me on the shin. Left a bruise the size of a saucer, all purple and yellow. It throbbed for a few days, especially when I was climbing the stairs to the second floor of the branch, but it faded eventually. Just a silly, clumsy mistake, really. Certainly nothing that required a doctor's attention. So, no, physically, aside from this terrible fatigue and the sleep issues, I've been completely fine. No recent illnesses, no injuries to speak of. Just... just this awful, heavy feeling in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you started any new medications or supplements recently?\n\nPatient: New medications or supplements? Oh, no, nothing new at all. I am very particular about what I take, you know. I mentioned my multivitamin earlier, the one with the extra D3, and I've been taking that same brand for... oh, it must be fifteen years now. I buy it at the local pharmacy, the one on Elm Street, not those big chain stores. I like to support local businesses when I can, and Mr. Henderson, the pharmacist there, always makes sure they have it in stock for me. \n\nBut as for anything new, no. I don't like to upset the apple cart, so to speak. My system is used to what it's used to. I mean, I see all those advertisements on the television, especially during the evening news, pushing this pill for that problem, and that powder for another thing... it's all just too much. I always think, who knows how all those things interact with each other? It just seems like asking for trouble. \n\nEven when I had that nasty bruise on my shin from the rake incident I told you about, I didn't even take an ibuprofen. I just put a cold compress on it—a bag of frozen peas wrapped in a clean dish towel, the blue and white checkered one, to be exact—and elevated my leg on the ottoman while I read. I prefer to let the body heal itself whenever possible, rather than throwing a bunch of new chemicals at it. So, to answer your question, no. My medicine cabinet looks exactly the same today as it did five years ago. Just the vitamins, some bandages, and a bottle of rubbing alcohol. Nothing new whatsoever.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I mean, I see all those advertisements on the television, especially during the evening news, pushing this pill for that problem, and that powder for another thing... it's all just too much. I always think, who knows how all those things interact with each other? It just seems like asking for trouble. \n\nEven when I had that nasty bruise on my shin from the rake incident I told you about, I didn't even take an ibuprofen. I just put a cold compress on it—a bag of frozen peas wrapped in a clean dish towel, the blue and white checkered one, to be exact—and elevated my leg on the ottoman while I read. I prefer to let the body heal itself whenever possible, rather than throwing a bunch of new chemicals at it. So, to answer your question, no. My medicine cabinet looks exactly the same today as it did five years ago. Just the vitamins, some bandages, and a bottle of rubbing alcohol. Nothing new whatsoever.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having trouble with your memory?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 60 / library_assistant_clerical / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Diminished_Thinking\nmanifestation: \"I came in because my mind feels foggy, and I cannot even keep my place in my old cataloguing notes or decide what simple thing to do next.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Trouble with my memory? Well, it's... it's not exactly that I'm forgetting my own name or where I live, or forgetting Mrs. Gable's face next door, no. But there is definitely something wrong up there right now. It's actually one of the main reasons I finally decided I needed to come in and talk to someone. My mind just feels so incredibly foggy lately. It's like trying to think through a bowl of thick, cold oatmeal, if that makes any sense. 

I used to be so sharp at the library—I mean, you have to be, especially when you're dealing with the older reference materials and the local history archives. But lately... well, I'll sit down at my desk with my old cataloguing notes, the handwritten ones I've kept for years to track the donations, and I just... I cannot even keep my place in them. I'll read the same line three or four times, tracing it with my finger, and the words just don't sink in. I'll look away for just a second to grab my pen—the blue Pilot gel pen I always use—and when I look back at the page, I've completely lost my train of thought. It's terribly frustrating, because I take a lot of pride in my accuracy.

And it's not just at work, either. It happens right in my own home with the most mundane things. I'll be standing in my kitchen, trying to figure out my morning routine, and I just can't decide what simple thing to do next. Should I wash my teacup first, or should I wipe down the counter? Should I open the blinds or feed the birds? Such simple little choices, right? Things I've done thousands of times. But I just stand there, staring at the sink, completely unable to make a decision. The gears just stop turning entirely. So, I wouldn't call it amnesia or anything dramatic like you see in the movies, but this heavy fog... it's just making it impossible to think straight or get through my normal day.
