I haven't been to the hospital for anything serious since... oh, gosh, it must have been the late nineties when I had a rather nasty bout of kidney stones. Now, that was an ordeal. I wouldn't wish that on my worst enemy, let me tell you. I remember I was at the library, actually, sorting through some new acquisitions in the reference section, and this pain just hit me out of nowhere. I thought it was my back at first, you know, from lifting those heavy encyclopedias, but it just kept getting worse and worse. I had to have my supervisor, Brenda—she's retired now, moved down to Florida with her sister—drive me to the emergency room. \n\nBut recently? No, nothing like that at all. I haven't even had a bad cold this winter, which is surprising because usually, the library is just a breeding ground for germs. All those people coming in and out, coughing on the keyboards, bringing in books they've had sitting on their nightstands while they were sick with the flu... you really have to be careful. I always make sure to wash my hands thoroughly, humming the 'Happy Birthday' song twice, just like they say. And I take my daily multivitamin, the one with the extra D3, because, as I mentioned, those dark winter evenings can really take a toll. \n\nI did have a bit of a scare last month, though, just a minor one. I was out back, putting down some extra mulch around the hostas before the first frost hit—I use a cedar blend, it really helps keep the moisture in and deters those awful slugs—and I stepped on a rake I had foolishly left lying in the grass. It popped right up and smacked me on the shin. Left a bruise the size of a saucer, all purple and yellow. It throbbed for a few days, especially when I was climbing the stairs to the second floor of the branch, but it faded eventually. Just a silly, clumsy mistake, really. Certainly nothing that required a doctor's attention. So, no, physically, aside from this terrible fatigue and the sleep issues, I've been completely fine. No recent illnesses, no injuries to speak of. Just... just this awful, heavy feeling in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you started any new medications or supplements recently?\n\nPatient: New medications or supplements? Oh, no, nothing new at all. I am very particular about what I take, you know. I mentioned my multivitamin earlier, the one with the extra D3, and I've been taking that same brand for... oh, it must be fifteen years now. I buy it at the local pharmacy, the one on Elm Street, not those big chain stores. I like to support local businesses when I can, and Mr. Henderson, the pharmacist there, always makes sure they have it in stock for me. \n\nBut as for anything new, no. I don't like to upset the apple cart, so to speak. My system is used to what it's used to. I mean, I see all those advertisements on the television, especially during the evening news, pushing this pill for that problem, and that powder for another thing... it's all just too much. I always think, who knows how all those things interact with each other? It just seems like asking for trouble. \n\nEven when I had that nasty bruise on my shin from the rake incident I told you about, I didn't even take an ibuprofen. I just put a cold compress on it—a bag of frozen peas wrapped in a clean dish towel, the blue and white checkered one, to be exact—and elevated my leg on the ottoman while I read. I prefer to let the body heal itself whenever possible, rather than throwing a bunch of new chemicals at it. So, to answer your question, no. My medicine cabinet looks exactly the same today as it did five years ago. Just the vitamins, some bandages, and a bottle of rubbing alcohol. Nothing new whatsoever.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: Trouble with my memory? Well, it's... it's not exactly that I'm forgetting my own name or where I live, or forgetting Mrs. Gable's face next door, no. But there is definitely something wrong up there right now. It's actually one of the main reasons I finally decided I needed to come in and talk to someone. My mind just feels so incredibly foggy lately. It's like trying to think through a bowl of thick, cold oatmeal, if that makes any sense. \n\nI used to be so sharp at the library—I mean, you have to be, especially when you're dealing with the older reference materials and the local history archives. But lately... well, I'll sit down at my desk with my old cataloguing notes, the handwritten ones I've kept for years to track the donations, and I just... I cannot even keep my place in them. I'll read the same line three or four times, tracing it with my finger, and the words just don't sink in. I'll look away for just a second to grab my pen—the blue Pilot gel pen I always use—and when I look back at the page, I've completely lost my train of thought. It's terribly frustrating, because I take a lot of pride in my accuracy.\n\nAnd it's not just at work, either. It happens right in my own home with the most mundane things. I'll be standing in my kitchen, trying to figure out my morning routine, and I just can't decide what simple thing to do next. Should I wash my teacup first, or should I wipe down the counter? Should I open the blinds or feed the birds? Such simple little choices, right? Things I've done thousands of times. But I just stand there, staring at the sink, completely unable to make a decision. The gears just stop turning entirely. So, I wouldn't call it amnesia or anything dramatic like you see in the movies, but this heavy fog... it's just making it impossible to think straight or get through my normal day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting confused about what day or date it is?\n\nPatient: Confused about the day or date? Oh, no, no, I haven't lost track of time like that. I mean, my mind is certainly a bit of a swamp right now, as I was just saying, but I still know my calendar. I've always been very meticulous about dates. You sort of have to be in my line of work, keeping track of due dates, interlibrary loan periods, staff schedules... it all requires a firm grasp on the calendar. \n\nI have this wonderful planner—it's a beautiful, leather-bound one that my niece, Sarah, bought for me a few Christmases ago. She knows how much I appreciate stationery. It has the days of the week on the left side and a blank page for notes on the right, which is just perfect for my lists. I sit down every Sunday evening, right after I watch my nature documentary on PBS, and I fill out the entire week. I write down my shifts at the library, when the recycling goes out—which is alternating Thursdays in my neighborhood, they just changed the schedule last year and it caused such an uproar at the neighborhood association meeting—and even little reminders to water my indoor ferns. \n\nSo, I am always very aware of exactly what day it is. Even with this awful fog in my head, I still check my planner first thing in the morning when I'm having my tea. I might stare at it for ten minutes trying to figure out if I have the energy to actually *do* the things I wrote down, but I know it's Tuesday, or the 14th, or whatever it happens to be. I haven't found myself waking up and thinking it's Saturday when it's really Wednesday, or anything alarming like that. The days are just... they're just passing by incredibly slowly right now, dragging on and on because everything is such an effort. But I do know which day is which.\n\nDoctor: Have you gotten lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Lost? In familiar places? Oh, goodness, no. No, I haven't experienced anything like that. I mean, my world is fairly small, to be perfectly honest with you. I have my routine, and I stick to it quite rigidly, especially lately when just getting out the door feels like such a monumental task. \n\nI take the exact same route to the library every single day. I walk down Maple Avenue, past the old Miller house—the one with the gorgeous wrap-around porch that they finally painted that lovely shade of sage green last summer, after years of it just peeling and looking so sad—and then I take a left onto Oak Street, right by the bakery. I can smell the sourdough bread baking every morning, usually right around 7:45. It's a very specific, comforting smell. I've walked that route for twenty-two years. I could probably do it blindfolded, or walking backwards. \n\nEven when I go to the grocery store, I go to the same Piggly Wiggly on 4th Street. I park in the same general area, usually near the cart return by the light pole, and I walk the aisles in the exact same order. Produce first, then the dairy, then down the dry goods... I know exactly where everything is. If they move the canned tomatoes, which they did about three months ago, moving them from aisle four to aisle six, well, that throws me off for a moment, certainly. I had to ask young Timmy, the stock boy, where they went. But I didn't forget where I was, or how to get home, or anything like that. \n\nSo, no, I haven't found myself wandering around my own neighborhood wondering where my house is, or getting lost in the stacks at the library. The fog in my head makes it hard to concentrate on what I'm *doing* in those places, as I mentioned, but the geography of my life... no, that's all still very firmly in place. I know exactly where I am. It's just... being there feels so incredibly heavy right now. But no, no getting lost.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Loss of appetite? Yes, actually, I have. It's... well, it's quite noticeable, at least to my wife, Martha. You see, I've always been a very hearty eater. Growing up, my mother instilled in us this absolute rule that you never, ever waste food. We weren't wealthy by any means, so if there was food on your plate, you finished it, down to the last grain of rice. And I carried that with me my whole life. I've always enjoyed my meals, especially a good, hot bowl of soup in the winter. Martha makes this incredible chicken and wild rice soup—she uses the recipe her grandmother brought over from Minnesota, simmering the broth for hours with celery and carrots and just a hint of thyme. It's wonderful.

But lately... I don't know, food just seems so unappealing. It's almost like ash in my mouth. I'll sit down at the dinner table, and Martha will have set out a beautiful meal, and I just look at it. I actually have to consciously remind myself that I need to eat, that my body requires fuel to keep going, even if I don't feel like doing anything. I'll take a few bites of the soup, maybe a spoonful or two of the rice, and then I just... I push the bowl away. It's half-finished, sometimes barely touched. 

Martha, bless her heart, she notices everything. She started pointing it out last week. She said, "Arthur, you're leaving perfectly good food in your bowl, and that is just not like you." And she's right, it isn't. I would never normally waste a meal. But the thought of chewing and swallowing... it just feels like too much effort, just like everything else right now. I just have no desire for it whatsoever. I think I've probably dropped a few pounds because of it, though I haven't stepped on the scale in the bathroom to check. The scale is tucked away under the linen cabinet, and pulling it out just seems like another chore I don't have the energy for. But yes, my appetite is just completely gone.

Like back in, oh, it must have been 2015, when they restructured the county library system and I thought I might be transferred to the downtown branch. I was terribly worried about the commute—the parking situation down there is just atrocious, you have to pay by the hour or walk six blocks from the municipal lot—and I remember I didn't sleep well for a few days, felt a bit low and anxious about the whole ordeal. Everything is just so incredibly flat.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling worthless?\n\nPatient: Worthless? Well, no, I... I don't think I'd use that word, exactly. I mean, I know I have worth. I've always been a very steady sort of person, you know? I've worked at the county library branch for, gosh, it will be twenty-two years this coming October. And I know they rely on me. Well, they usually rely on me, before my brain turned to mush like I was saying earlier. But I know my value as a person. I'm a good neighbor, I always make sure the snow is shoveled on Mrs. Gable's side of the duplex—she's eighty-four, you see, and can't manage the heavy wet snow we get around here. So, I know I contribute. I don't sit around thinking I'm a bad person or a waste of space or anything severe like that. \n\nIt's really more just... well, it's like I was saying about the flatness. I feel empty, maybe, or useless in the moment because I can't seem to get my chores done or sort my catalog cards, but not worthless in the grand scheme of things. Does that make sense? I'll sit in my armchair and think about my life, and I know I've done alright for a guy with just a high school diploma who never really went off to do big, grand, exciting things. I've lived a quiet life, but a very decent one. I don't hate myself. I don't have those kinds of dark thoughts about my character. It's just... I just don't have the energy to be the person I know I am right now. So, no, not worthless. Just... stalled out, I suppose you could say.\n\nDoctor: Before this current period, have you ever had another time in your life when you felt depressed like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: Well, gosh, before this? No, I really don't think so. I mean, life has its normal ups and downs, doesn't it? You know, you have a bad week here or there. Like back in, oh, it must have been 2015, when they restructured the county library system and I thought I might be transferred to the downtown branch. I was terribly worried about the commute—the parking situation down there is just atrocious, you have to pay by the hour or walk six blocks from the municipal lot—and I remember I didn't sleep well for a few days, felt a bit low and anxious about the whole ordeal. But nothing like this. \n\nThis... this heavy, gray blanket that just sits on me day after day, week after week... no, this is entirely new territory for me. I've always prided myself on being a very even-keeled sort of fellow. You know, steady. I show up, I do my work, I tend my hostas. Sure, maybe I've had a day or two of feeling under the weather or a bit blue in the winter months when the sun sets at four-thirty in the afternoon. I've always hated that, locking up the branch and driving home in the pitch dark, it makes the days feel so short. But two whole weeks of just... nothingness? Of not even wanting to look at my watercolors or do the Sunday puzzle? No, doctor. Never anything quite like this. \n\nIt's frankly a bit frightening, to be honest with you, because I'm just not used to my mind feeling so... so stuck. I keep waiting to just snap out of it, like you would a common cold. I keep telling myself, 'Tomorrow morning, you'll wake up and make your coffee and want to read your book,' but then tomorrow comes and it's just the same flat feeling all over again. It just keeps dragging on. So, no, to answer your question, this is really the first time it's been this bad for this long.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been lately?\n\nPatient: Sleep? Well, it has been quite frustrating, to be completely honest with you. I mean, by the time evening rolls around, I am just so terribly exhausted. That heavy, flat feeling I was telling you about, it just wears me right down to the bone. I go through my usual evening routine, you know, I make myself a cup of herbal tea—usually peppermint, I find chamomile a bit too earthy—and I make sure the deadbolt is locked, and I get into bed right around ten-thirty. And I am tired. I really am. I pull the covers up and I am just so ready to be asleep and escape the day. \n\nBut then, it never fails, my eyes just snap wide open. I roll over and look at the digital clock on the nightstand—it is one of those older models with the bright red numbers, I have had it for absolute ages, since the nineties at least—and it is always right around four in the morning. Four-fifteen, three-fifty-eight, right in that dreadful, quiet pocket of the night. And that is it for me. Sleep is just entirely gone. \n\nI do not get up, though. It is far too early to start the coffee maker, and it is pitch black outside, so I just lie there. I lie there staring up at the shadow of the ceiling fan, and my mind just starts churning. Not fast, mind you, just a slow, heavy sort of turning over. I just lie there thinking about what I am supposed to do with myself now. I look ahead at the whole empty day stretching out in front of me, thinking about the hours I have to fill when I have absolutely no desire to do any of my usual things. It is a very long, very lonely time to be awake, just trapped there in the dark wondering how on earth I am going to get through another day of feeling this way. So yes, sleep has been very poor indeed.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life is not worth living?\n\nPatient: Life not worth living? Oh, gosh, no. No, I wouldn't say that at all. I mean, I know things are very difficult right now, and I was just telling you about those awful mornings lying awake at four a.m., dreading the day. But that's just because the days feel so long and heavy right now, not because I want to give up on life itself. \n\nI actually have a lot of respect for life, you know? I think about my parents—they passed away some time ago, my mother in 2008 and my father a few years before that—and they worked so hard to give me a good, stable upbringing. They went through the Depression and all sorts of hardships, and they always taught me to value what you have, even when things are tough. So, I would never just throw that away. \n\nAnd besides, I have my hostas to tend to when spring comes around, and Mrs. Gable next door, she really does rely on me, even if it's just to bring her mail up to the porch when the steps get icy. I know my life isn't some big, dramatic adventure, but it's mine, and I've built a comfortable little corner for myself here. I just want to feel like myself again in it, you see? I just want this gray fog to lift so I can go back to enjoying my peppermint tea and my Sunday puzzles. So, no, doctor, I don't have thoughts like that. I just want to feel better, not... not end things. That's not me at all.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any recent medical illness or injury?\n\nPatient: Medical illness or injury? Oh, no, no. I've been quite fortunate in that regard, thankfully. I mean, my health has always been relatively sturdy. I haven't been to the hospital for anything serious since... oh, gosh, it must have been the late nineties when I had a rather nasty bout of kidney stones. Now, that was an ordeal. I wouldn't wish that on my worst enemy, let me tell you. I remember I was at the library, actually, sorting through some new acquisitions in the reference section, and this pain just hit me out of nowhere. I thought it was my back at first, you know, from lifting those heavy encyclopedias, but it just kept getting worse and worse. I had to have my supervisor, Brenda—she's retired now, moved down to Florida with her sister—drive me to the emergency room. \n\nBut recently? No, nothing like that at all. I haven't even had a bad cold this winter, which is surprising because usually, the library is just a breeding ground for germs. All those people coming in and out, coughing on the keyboards, bringing in books they've had sitting on their nightstands while they were sick with the flu... you really have to be careful. I always make sure to wash my hands thoroughly, humming the 'Happy Birthday' song twice, just like they say. And I take my daily multivitamin, the one with the extra D3, because, as I mentioned, those dark winter evenings can really take a toll. \n\nI did have a bit of a scare last month, though, just a minor one. I was out back, putting down some extra mulch around the hostas before the first frost hit—I use a cedar blend, it really helps keep the moisture in and deters those awful slugs—and I stepped on a rake I had foolishly left lying in the grass. It popped right up and smacked me on the shin. Left a bruise the size of a saucer, all purple and yellow. It throbbed for a few days, especially when I was climbing the stairs to the second floor of the branch, but it faded eventually. Just a silly, clumsy mistake, really. Certainly nothing that required a doctor's attention. So, no, physically, aside from this terrible fatigue and the sleep issues, I've been completely fine. No recent illnesses, no injuries to speak of. Just... just this awful, heavy feeling in my mind.\n\nDoctor: Have you started any new medications or supplements recently?\n\nPatient: New medications or supplements? Oh, no, nothing new at all. I am very particular about what I take, you know. I mentioned my multivitamin earlier, the one with the extra D3, and I've been taking that same brand for... oh, it must be fifteen years now. I buy it at the local pharmacy, the one on Elm Street, not those big chain stores. I like to support local businesses when I can, and Mr. Henderson, the pharmacist there, always makes sure they have it in stock for me. \n\nBut as for anything new, no. I don't like to upset the apple cart, so to speak. My system is used to what it's used to. I mean, I see all those advertisements on the television, especially during the evening news, pushing this pill for that problem, and that powder for another thing... it's all just too much. I always think, who knows how all those things interact with each other? It just seems like asking for trouble. \n\nEven when I had that nasty bruise on my shin from the rake incident I told you about, I didn't even take an ibuprofen. I just put a cold compress on it—a bag of frozen peas wrapped in a clean dish towel, the blue and white checkered one, to be exact—and elevated my leg on the ottoman while I read. I prefer to let the body heal itself whenever possible, rather than throwing a bunch of new chemicals at it. So, to answer your question, no. My medicine cabinet looks exactly the same today as it did five years ago. Just the vitamins, some bandages, and a bottle of rubbing alcohol. Nothing new whatsoever.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble with your memory?\n\nPatient: Trouble with my memory? Well, it's... it's not exactly that I'm forgetting my own name or where I live, or forgetting Mrs. Gable's face next door, no. But there is definitely something wrong up there right now. It's actually one of the main reasons I finally decided I needed to come in and talk to someone. My mind just feels so incredibly foggy lately. It's like trying to think through a bowl of thick, cold oatmeal, if that makes any sense. \n\nI used to be so sharp at the library—I mean, you have to be, especially when you're dealing with the older reference materials and the local history archives. But lately... well, I'll sit down at my desk with my old cataloguing notes, the handwritten ones I've kept for years to track the donations, and I just... I cannot even keep my place in them. I'll read the same line three or four times, tracing it with my finger, and the words just don't sink in. I'll look away for just a second to grab my pen—the blue Pilot gel pen I always use—and when I look back at the page, I've completely lost my train of thought. It's terribly frustrating, because I take a lot of pride in my accuracy.\n\nAnd it's not just at work, either. It happens right in my own home with the most mundane things. I'll be standing in my kitchen, trying to figure out my morning routine, and I just can't decide what simple thing to do next. Should I wash my teacup first, or should I wipe down the counter? Should I open the blinds or feed the birds? Such simple little choices, right? Things I've done thousands of times. But I just stand there, staring at the sink, completely unable to make a decision. The gears just stop turning entirely. So, I wouldn't call it amnesia or anything dramatic like you see in the movies, but this heavy fog... it's just making it impossible to think straight or get through my normal day.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting confused about what day or date it is?\n\nPatient: Confused about the day or date? Oh, no, no, I haven't lost track of time like that. I mean, my mind is certainly a bit of a swamp right now, as I was just saying, but I still know my calendar. I've always been very meticulous about dates. You sort of have to be in my line of work, keeping track of due dates, interlibrary loan periods, staff schedules... it all requires a firm grasp on the calendar. \n\nI have this wonderful planner—it's a beautiful, leather-bound one that my niece, Sarah, bought for me a few Christmases ago. She knows how much I appreciate stationery. It has the days of the week on the left side and a blank page for notes on the right, which is just perfect for my lists. I sit down every Sunday evening, right after I watch my nature documentary on PBS, and I fill out the entire week. I write down my shifts at the library, when the recycling goes out—which is alternating Thursdays in my neighborhood, they just changed the schedule last year and it caused such an uproar at the neighborhood association meeting—and even little reminders to water my indoor ferns. \n\nSo, I am always very aware of exactly what day it is. Even with this awful fog in my head, I still check my planner first thing in the morning when I'm having my tea. I might stare at it for ten minutes trying to figure out if I have the energy to actually *do* the things I wrote down, but I know it's Tuesday, or the 14th, or whatever it happens to be. I haven't found myself waking up and thinking it's Saturday when it's really Wednesday, or anything alarming like that. The days are just... they're just passing by incredibly slowly right now, dragging on and on because everything is such an effort. But I do know which day is which.\n\nDoctor: Have you gotten lost in places that should be familiar to you?\n\nPatient: Lost? In familiar places? Oh, goodness, no. No, I haven't experienced anything like that. I mean, my world is fairly small, to be perfectly honest with you. I have my routine, and I stick to it quite rigidly, especially lately when just getting out the door feels like such a monumental task. \n\nI take the exact same route to the library every single day. I walk down Maple Avenue, past the old Miller house—the one with the gorgeous wrap-around porch that they finally painted that lovely shade of sage green last summer, after years of it just peeling and looking so sad—and then I take a left onto Oak Street, right by the bakery. I can smell the sourdough bread baking every morning, usually right around 7:45. It's a very specific, comforting smell. I've walked that route for twenty-two years. I could probably do it blindfolded, or walking backwards. \n\nEven when I go to the grocery store, I go to the same Piggly Wiggly on 4th Street. I park in the same general area, usually near the cart return by the light pole, and I walk the aisles in the exact same order. Produce first, then the dairy, then down the dry goods... I know exactly where everything is. If they move the canned tomatoes, which they did about three months ago, moving them from aisle four to aisle six, well, that throws me off for a moment, certainly. I had to ask young Timmy, the stock boy, where they went. But I didn't forget where I was, or how to get home, or anything like that. \n\nSo, no, I haven't found myself wandering around my own neighborhood wondering where my house is, or getting lost in the stacks at the library. The fog in my head makes it hard to concentrate on what I'm *doing* in those places, as I mentioned, but the geography of my life... no, that's all still very firmly in place. I know exactly where I am. It's just... being there feels so incredibly heavy right now. But no, no getting lost.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Loss of appetite? Yes, actually, I have. It's... well, it's quite noticeable, at least to my wife, Martha. You see, I've always been a very hearty eater. Growing up, my mother instilled in us this absolute rule that you never, ever waste food. We weren't wealthy by any means, so if there was food on your plate, you finished it, down to the last grain of rice. And I carried that with me my whole life. I've always enjoyed my meals, especially a good, hot bowl of soup in the winter. Martha makes this incredible chicken and wild rice soup—she uses the recipe her grandmother brought over from Minnesota, simmering the broth for hours with celery and carrots and just a hint of thyme. It's wonderful.\n\nBut lately... I don't know, food just seems so unappealing. It's almost like ash in my mouth. I'll sit down at the dinner table, and Martha will have set out a beautiful meal, and I just look at it. I actually have to consciously remind myself that I need to eat, that my body requires fuel to keep going, even if I don't feel like doing anything. I'll take a few bites of the soup, maybe a spoonful or two of the rice, and then I just... I push the bowl away. It's half-finished, sometimes barely touched. \n\nMartha, bless her heart, she notices everything. She started pointing it out last week. She said, \"Arthur, you're leaving perfectly good food in your bowl, and that is just not like you.\" And she's right, it isn't. I would never normally waste a meal. But the thought of chewing and swallowing... it just feels like too much effort, just like everything else right now. I just have no desire for it whatsoever. I think I've probably dropped a few pounds because of it, though I haven't stepped on the scale in the bathroom to check. The scale is tucked away under the linen cabinet, and pulling it out just seems like another chore I don't have the energy for. But yes, my appetite is just completely gone."
